<div class='question question'>

Divide: $78 \div 6$

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

$
\begin{aligned}
&= 78 \div 6 \\\\
&= \dfrac {78} {6} \\\\
&= \dfrac {13 \times 6} {6} \\\\
&= \dfrac {13 \times \cancel {6}} {\cancel {6}} \\\\
&= 13
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$13$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$
\begin{aligned}
&= 78 \div 6 \\\\
&= \dfrac {78} {6} \\\\
&= \dfrac {13 \times 6} {6} \\\\
&= \dfrac {13 \times \cancel {6}} {\cancel {6}} \\\\
&= 13
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<p class='review_status initial' id='SUBTAG-STATUS-CEFEC184-01C9-49CB-9761-F2A3E70E6B6F'>Initial</p>
<div class='question question'>

Write down two numbers that add to $14$ and multiply to $48$. 

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the first number be $a$ and second be $b$.

As per question,

$
\begin{aligned}
a + b      &= 14 \\
a \times b &= 48
\end{aligned}
$

Let's find $a$ and $b$.

$
\begin{aligned}
&= x^2 + 14x + 48 \\\\
&= x^2 + (6 + 8)x + (6 \times 8) \\\\
&= (x + 6) (x+ 8)
\end{aligned}
$

$x = 6$ and $x = 8$

the two numbers are $6$ and $8$

checking: 

$
\begin{aligned}
&- a + b   \\   
&= 6 + 8 \\
&= 14 \\\\
&= a \times b \\
&= 6 \times 8
&= 48 
\end{aligned}
$


</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$6$ and $8$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the first number be $a$ and second be $b$.

As per question,

$
\begin{aligned}
a + b      &= 14 \\
a \times b &= 48
\end{aligned}
$

Let's find $a$ and $b$.

$
\begin{aligned}
&= x^2 + 14x + 48 \\\\
&= x^2 + (6 + 8)x + (6 \times 8) \\\\
&= (x + 6) (x+ 8)
\end{aligned}
$

$x = 6$ and $x = 8$

the two numbers are $6$ and $8$

checking: 

$
\begin{aligned}
&- a + b   \\   
&= 6 + 8 \\
&= 14 \\\\
&= a \times b \\
&= 6 \times 8
&= 48 
\end{aligned}
$

$
\begin{aligned}
&= 21 - 5 \times 2 + 6 \\\\
&= 21 - 10 + 6 \\\\
&= 11 + 6 \\\\
&= 17
\end{aligned}
$

</div>

$17$

\end{aligned}
$

<p class='review_status initial' id='SUBTAG-STATUS-872AF346-F083-464D-8D78-F9ED85CC0CD9'>Initial</p>
<div class='question question'>

Alison, Bethany and Catherine are three sisters. 
They are $6$ years old, $7$ years old and $12$ years old. 

Bethany is older than Alison. 
Catherine's age is a prime number. 

What is each girls' age?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

As Catherine’s age is a prime number,
Catherine is $ 7 \ \text{years}$ old.

Bethany is older than Alison,
so Bethany is $12 \ \text {years}$ old.

Hence, Alison is $6 \ \text {years}$ old.

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

Alison $=$ $6 \ \text {years}$

</div>
<div class='answer'>

Bethany $=$ $12 \ \text {years}$

</div>
<div class='answer'>

Catherine $=$ $7 \ \text {years}$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>As Catherine’s age is a prime number,
Catherine is $ 7 \ \text{years}$ old.

Bethany is older than Alison,
so Bethany is $12 \ \text {years}$ old.

Hence, Alison is $6 \ \text {years}$ old.
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Alison</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$6 \ \text {years}$
</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Bethany</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$12 \ \text {years}$
</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>

$
\begin{aligned}
&=8 - (5+2) \\\\
&=8 - (7)   \\\\
&= 1         \\\\
&= \text{L.H.S} \\\\
\text {L.H.S} &= \text {R.H.S} \\\\
\end{aligned}
$

<p class='review_status initial' id='SUBTAG-STATUS-0AB7CD26-587D-4215-AC2C-165A51DF4594'>Initial</p>
<div class='question question'>

From Year $10$ all pupils must join the Combined Cadet Force ("CCF") or opt for School Community Service 
("SCS"). Pupils can not opt for both CCF and SCS.

Rachel asked $150$ Year $9$ pupils whether they wanted to opt for CCF or SCS next year.

$60\%$ of the pupils said that they wanted to opt for CCF.

$\dfrac{1}{6}$ of the pupils said that they wanted to opt for SCS.

The rest of the pupils said that they hadn't yet decided.

How many pupils hadn't yet decided whether to do CCF or carry out SCS next year?

<p class='review_status initial' id='SUBTAG-STATUS-CE041EF6-D2B5-42A8-8F9B-E36FA1D03E08'>Initial</p>
<div class='question question'>

Sam thinks of a number.

He multiplies that number by $5$. 

Then he subtracts $12$. 

Then he divides by $3$. 

Finally he adds $17$.

His answer is $38$.

What number did Sam originally think of?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the number that Sam thought of $= a$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
\dfrac {(5a -12)}{3} + 17                                    &= 38 \\\\
\dfrac {5a -12}{3}                                           &= 38 - 17\\\\
\dfrac {5a -12}{3}                                           &= 21 \\\\
5a - 12                                                      &= 21 \times 3 \\\\
5a - 12                                                      &= 63 \\\\  
5a                                                           &= 63 + 12 \\\\ 
5a                                                           &= 75 \\\\
a                                                            &= 15
\end{aligned}
$

The number that Sam thought of $= 15$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$15$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the number that Sam thought of $= a$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
\dfrac {(5a -12)}{3} + 17                                    &= 38 \\\\
\dfrac {5a -12}{3}                                           &= 38 - 17\\\\
\dfrac {5a -12}{3}                                           &= 21 \\\\
5a - 12                                                      &= 21 \times 3 \\\\
5a - 12                                                      &= 63 \\\\  
5a                                                           &= 63 + 12 \\\\ 
5a                                                           &= 75 \\\\
a                                                            &= 15
\end{aligned}
$

The number that Sam thought of $= 15$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$15$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<p class='review_status initial' id='SUBTAG-STATUS-F1F1A10E-00DD-4BD6-8869-3317F652EBF6'>Initial</p>
<div class='question question'>

Stuart's patio is $5 \ \text{metres}$ long and $5 \ \text{metres}$ wide.

Stuart wants to cover the patio with paving stones. Each paving stone is $50 \ \text{centimetres}$ long and 
$50 \ \text{centimetres}$ wide.

How many paving stones does Stuart need to buy?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

ABBR: $\text{Area of rectangle} = \text{length} \times \text{width}$


ABBR: It is really important to use the right units in calculations and convert where required
.
The patio measurement is given in $\text{meters}$ while the stone measurement is given in $\text{cm}$.

Let's convert the patio measurements into $\text{cm}$.

As per the question,

$
\begin {aligned}
\text {Number of stones} &= \dfrac {\text{Area of the patio}} {\text{Area of a stone}} \\\\
                         &= \dfrac {5 \ \text{m} \times 5 \ \text{m}} {50 \ \text{cm} \times 50 \ \text{cm}} \\\\
                         &= \dfrac {5 \times 100 \ \text{cm} \times 5 \times 100 \ \text{cm}} {50 \ \text{cm} \times 50 \ \text{cm}} \\\\
                         &= \dfrac {5 \times 100 \times 5 \times 100} {50  \times 50} \\\\
                         &= \dfrac {5 \times 10 \times 10 \times 5 \times 10 \times 10} {5 \times 10 \times 5 \times 10} \\\\
                         &= \dfrac {\cancel {5} \times \cancel {10} \times 10 \times \cancel {5} \times \cancel {10} \times 10} {\cancel {5} \times \cancel {10} \times \cancel {5} \times \cancel {10}} \\\\
                         &= 10 \times 10 \\\\
                         &= 100
\end {aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$100$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>rtq_abbr_katex_formula_area_rectangle_expanded

rtq_abbr_md_unit_no_dot_note.
The patio measurement is given in $\text{meters}$ while the stone measurement is given in $\text{cm}$.

Let's convert the patio measurements into $\text{cm}$.

As per the question,

$
\begin {aligned}
\text {Number of stones} &= \dfrac {\text{Area of the patio}} {\text{Area of a stone}} \\\\
                         &= \dfrac {5 \ \text{m} \times 5 \ \text{m}} {50 \ \text{cm} \times 50 \ \text{cm}} \\\\
                         &= \dfrac {5 \times 100 \ \text{cm} \times 5 \times 100 \ \text{cm}} {50 \ \text{cm} \times 50 \ \text{cm}} \\\\
                         &= \dfrac {5 \times 100 \times 5 \times 100} {50  \times 50} \\\\
                         &= \dfrac {5 \times 10 \times 10 \times 5 \times 10 \times 10} {5 \times 10 \times 5 \times 10} \\\\
                         &= \dfrac {\cancel {5} \times \cancel {10} \times 10 \times \cancel {5} \times \cancel {10} \times 10} {\cancel {5} \times \cancel {10} \times \cancel {5} \times \cancel {10}} \\\\
                         &= 10 \times 10 \\\\
                         &= 100
\end {aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$100$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<ul class='subquestion lower-alpha'>
<li>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

Stuart has $\pounds 200$ left after buying the paving stones. Stuart wants to put a picket fence around **three sides** of his patio.

Each panel of picket fencing is $1 \ \text{metre}$ long and costs $\pounds 10.50$. 
Stuart will also have to pay a delivery charge of $\pounds 5$ regardless of the number of panels of picket fencing he purchases.

How much money does Stuart have left after buying the picket fencing?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
\text {Number of panels}            &= 3 \times \dfrac {\text {Length of a side}} {\text {Length of a panel}} \\\\
                                    &= 3 \times \dfrac {5} {1} \\\\
                                    &= 3 \times 5 \\\\
                                    &= 15 \\\\
\text {Cost of panels}              &= 10.50 \times 15 \\\\
                                    &= \pounds 157.5 \\\\
\text {Money left}                  &= \text {Total Money} - \text {Cost of panels} - \text {Delivery charge} \\\\
                                    &= 200 - 157.5 - 5 \\\\
                                    &= \pounds 37.5
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$\pounds 37.5$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
\text {Number of panels}            &= 3 \times \dfrac {\text {Length of a side}} {\text {Length of a panel}} \\\\
                                    &= 3 \times \dfrac {5} {1} \\\\
                                    &= 3 \times 5 \\\\
                                    &= 15 \\\\
\text {Cost of panels}              &= 10.50 \times 15 \\\\
                                    &= \pounds 157.5 \\\\
\text {Money left}                  &= \text {Total Money} - \text {Cost of panels} - \text {Delivery charge} \\\\
                                    &= 200 - 157.5 - 5 \\\\
                                    &= \pounds 37.5
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$\pounds 37.5$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

</div>
</li>
</ul>

<p class='review_status initial' id='SUBTAG-STATUS-453DC04D-CA8F-48C4-88A2-8AC9261FA908'>Initial</p>
<div class='question question'>

One angle of an isosceles triangle is $80 \degree$. 

What are the other angles? 

There are two possible solutions to this question.

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

ABBR: $\text{Sum of angles in a triangle} = 180 \degree$


Let the second angle $= a$

As the triangle is an isosceles triangle, the third angle $= a$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
a  + a + 80                             &= 180 \\\\
2a + 80                                 &= 180 \\\\
2a                                      &= 180 - 80 \\\\
2a                                      &= 100 \\\\
a                                       &= \dfrac {100} {2} \\\\
a                                       &= \dfrac {50 \times 2} {2} \\\\
a                                       &= \dfrac {50 \times \cancel {2}} {\cancel {2}} \\\\
a                                       &= 50 \degree
\end{aligned}
$

Second angle $= 50 \degree$

Third angle $= 50 \degree$

</div>
<div class='working'>

ABBR: $\text{Sum of angles in a triangle} = 180 \degree$


Let the second angle $= a$

As the triangle is an isosceles triangle, the third angle $= 80$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
a  + 80 + 80                            &= 180 \\\\
a + 160                                 &= 180 \\\\
a                                       &= 180 - 160 \\\\
a                                       &= 20 \degree
\end{aligned}
$

Second angle $= 20 \degree$

Third angle $= 80 \degree$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$50 \degree, 50 \degree$

</div>
<div class='answer'>

$20 \degree, 80 \degree$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>rtq_abbr_katex_formula_angles_of_triangle

Let the second angle $= a$

As the triangle is an isosceles triangle, the third angle $= a$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
a  + a + 80                             &= 180 \\\\
2a + 80                                 &= 180 \\\\
2a                                      &= 180 - 80 \\\\
2a                                      &= 100 \\\\
a                                       &= \dfrac {100} {2} \\\\
a                                       &= \dfrac {50 \times 2} {2} \\\\
a                                       &= \dfrac {50 \times \cancel {2}} {\cancel {2}} \\\\
a                                       &= 50 \degree
\end{aligned}
$

Second angle $= 50 \degree$

Third angle $= 50 \degree$
</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>rtq_abbr_katex_formula_angles_of_triangle

Let the second angle $= a$

As the triangle is an isosceles triangle, the third angle $= 80$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
a  + 80 + 80                            &= 180 \\\\
a + 160                                 &= 180 \\\\
a                                       &= 180 - 160 \\\\
a                                       &= 20 \degree
\end{aligned}
$

Second angle $= 20 \degree$

Third angle $= 80 \degree$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$50 \degree, 50 \degree$
</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>

</code></pre>

<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

What is the area of this triangle?

![missing image](/papers/missing_image.svg)


</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

ABBR: $\text{Area of triangle} = \dfrac{1}{2} \times \text{base} \times \text{height}$


As per the diagram,

$
\beign{aligned}
\text {Area}            &= \dfrac {1} {2} \times 5 \times 4 \\\\
                        &= \dfrac {5 \times 4} {2} \\\\
                        &= \dfrac {5 \times 2 \times 2} {2} \\\\
                        &= \dfrac {5 \times 2 \times \cancel {2}} {\cancel {2}} \\\\
                        &= 5 \times 2 \\\\
                        &= 10 \ \text {cm}^2
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$10 \ \text {cm}^2$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>rtq_abbr_katex_formula_area_triangle_expanded

As per the diagram,

$
\beign{aligned}
\text {Area}            &= \dfrac {1} {2} \times 5 \times 4 \\\\
                        &= \dfrac {5 \times 4} {2} \\\\
                        &= \dfrac {5 \times 2 \times 2} {2} \\\\
                        &= \dfrac {5 \times 2 \times \cancel {2}} {\cancel {2}} \\\\
                        &= 5 \times 2 \\\\
                        &= 10 \ \text {cm}^2
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<li>
<div class='question_envelope rag_not_found rag_not_found subquestion'>
<div class='question subquestion'>

By thinking of two different ways to work out the area of the triangle, calculate the length of arrow.

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

ABBR: $\text{Area of triangle} = \dfrac{1}{2} \times \text{base} \times \text{height}$


Let the length of the arrow $= h$

As, per the diagram,

$
\beign{aligned}
\dfrac {1} {2} \times 10 \times h        &= \dfrac {1} {2} \times 8 \times 6 \\\\
\dfrac {10 \times h} {2}                 &= \dfrac {8 \times 6} {2}  \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {8 \times 6 \times 2} {2 \times 10} \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {8 \times 3 \times 2 \times 2} {2 \times 10} \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {8 \times 3 \times 2 \times 2} {2 \times 2 \times 5} \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {8 \times 3 \times \cancel {2} \times \cancel {2}} {\cancel {2} \times \cancel {2} \times 5} \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {8 \times 3} {5} \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {24} {5} \\\\
h                                        &= 4.8 \ \text {cm}
\end{aligned}
$

Length of the arrow $= 4.8 \ \text {cm}$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$4.8 \ \text {cm}$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>rtq_abbr_katex_formula_area_triangle_expanded

Let the length of the arrow $= h$

As, per the diagram,

$
\beign{aligned}
\dfrac {1} {2} \times 10 \times h        &= \dfrac {1} {2} \times 8 \times 6 \\\\
\dfrac {10 \times h} {2}                 &= \dfrac {8 \times 6} {2}  \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {8 \times 6 \times 2} {2 \times 10} \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {8 \times 3 \times 2 \times 2} {2 \times 10} \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {8 \times 3 \times 2 \times 2} {2 \times 2 \times 5} \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {8 \times 3 \times \cancel {2} \times \cancel {2}} {\cancel {2} \times \cancel {2} \times 5} \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {8 \times 3} {5} \\\\
h                                        &= \dfrac {24} {5} \\\\
h                                        &= 4.8 \ \text {cm}
\end{aligned}
$

Length of the arrow $= 4.8 \ \text {cm}$
</code></pre>
</div>
